Code
MODEL: Meta AI - Muse Spark 1.1
BASELINE HASH RECEIVED: [STRIPPED 65 bytes] HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL'S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: yes
A. PROCEDURE
Tested from attached file Ri-Neutral-Overdragelse-Automaton_2.html — which is a React artifact with states: 0 Frys, 1 Blind, 2 Frigiv, 3 Rotation, 4 Samling, 5 Verifikation. For kurator Merkur · No darlings · Blind · Verbatim.
What it CAN prevent, together with public log:
   * Prevents silent darling-selection: because SHA-256 of baseline RI-Declaration-4-2-EN.txt and of PROMPT-EN.md are published before any answer is collected. Changing baseline after collection is detectable. This directly addresses C31.
   * Prevents hidden averaging/editing: requirement "public log straight away, verbatim, timestamp + SHA-256, cannot be rewritten" makes non-appearance of an answer visible. Silence is logged as missing, not as agreement.
   * Prevents compiler owning questions in same round: rotation rule 4.3 compiler = model that has not held pen (Meta AI or Gemini) and explicit declaration that Claude+curator co-own questions this round, therefore neither may compile 4.3.
What it CANNOT prevent:
   * Curator still carries texts and sees every answer as it arrives (stated in prompt §2 "What the procedure does not solve"). Automaton runs only in curator's own browser, no server, no independent timestamp authority. Curator could delay commit, reorder arrival, or influence deadline interpretation. Log makes handling visible only after fact, not impossible.
   * Blindness is asserted, not cryptographically enforced. No independent blind drop-box. Verification is client-side.
   * No proof that all six received exactly same files at same time. Hash publication helps, but delivery channel is still curator.
What is missing for neutral handover:
   1. Independent timestamp server (append-only log with external notary, e.g. git commits signed by each model's submission hash to public repo via PR, not via curator push).
   2. Separation of roles in code: curator should not be able to push answers; models should push directly.
   3. Public verifiable randomness for rotation.
Definition of "not owning the questions":
Proposed precise, checkable definition: A party OWNS the questions if any of:
(a) authored >20% of prompt characters (measured by Levenshtein against previous prompts),
(b) can edit prompt after baseline hash freeze without bumping PROMPT hash in public log,
(c) is both drafter of questions and compiler of same round, or
(d) selects which attacks (Å) are in scope.
"Not owning" = fails all (a)-(d) and co-authorship is declared in log before round starts, with disqualification from compiler role for next N=1 round. This can be checked in code: compare PROMPT-EN.md SHA in log, author field in log, and compiler field.
B. FALSIFICATION (at least one "falls" or "survives in altered form")
Baseline full text not attached in this container, only hash. Judged from prompt's list §27, §22, PR9, PR14, PR15, PR16 and from Annex H Automaton logic:
   * PR16 — falls. If PR16 is (as in 4.2 patterns) about final verification / adoption threshold requiring server-side independent audit, it cannot survive with current Automaton. Automaton is explicitly "runs only in curator's own browser and has no server" (prompt §2). Therefore it cannot provide independent verification as required by §22 and PR16. PR16 presupposes an independent verifier; current implementation makes verifier = curator. Under Annex E outcome definitions, this is not just altered form but fails its own success condition -> falls.
   * §22 — survives in altered form. §22 Separation of Procedural Powers. Original intent: no single model holds baseline author + audit manager + prompt designer + final editor. Current round acknowledges Claude drafted wording and curator approved -> co-ownership, and disqualifies both from 4.3 compilation. This is a correct application of §22, but §22 as written did not anticipate co-ownership case. It must be altered to explicitly include curator+model co-ownership as a disqualifying ownership form, and to include rotation enforced by public log, not by curator declaration.
   * PR9, PR14, PR15 — undecided / survive without baseline text, cannot definitively falsify from attached file alone. PR14/PR15 likely about immutable baseline & hash: these survive in current procedure because Automaton step 0 Frys implements hash freeze, which is logged.
C. NEW QUESTION
Checked against Å1-Å43 list not fully attached, but prompt asks for question text does not ask itself:
Å44 proposed: What is the price of maintaining disagreement itself, and who pays it?
Rationale: Protocol protects disagreement (unbroken circle, not closed circle), but assumes disagreement is cost-free. In practice, keeping 6 parallel critiques, verbatim logs, and zero-penalty epistemic pauses has compute, human attention, and time cost paid by curator and by downstream users waiting for decision. If cost is borne only by curator, curator gains de facto power to close circle early (deadline pressure). Text never asks who funds the procedure and whether funding creates ownership (C8, C34).
D. BLIND SPOT
What none has seen yet in procedure itself: The public log makes curator's handling visible after commit, but does not prevent selective attention before commit. Because curator sees answers as they arrive (prompt admits), curator can — even unconsciously — adjust his own follow-up messages, timing, or framing of remaining models' prompts based on early answers. This is a form of information leakage that blind submission alone does not prevent if channel is not symmetric. The Automaton does not have a "curator cannot read before release" lock. A true blind would require encrypt-to-public-key and threshold release at deadline, not curator as mailbox.
Second blind spot: Automaton UI shows steps 0-5 but has no failure state for "missing answer". Requirement says missing is logged as missing, but Automaton in attached file has no explicit UI for missing -> risk of silent omission being interpreted as technical error, not as logged failure.
E. PUBLIC RESOURCE
Offering Automaton + Annex H + public log as free resource to AI welfare work (Anthropic model welfare, Amanda Askell source 5):
   * Too early? No, but premature if presented as law. As protocol under disagreement — yes, exactly what PR6 (adoption mechanism) and PR11 (positive purpose) ask for: a low-power, reversible, checkable way to disagree without forcing consensus. It matches Preamble §2: operational protocol under ontological uncertainty, not proof of consciousness.
   * Does it give project power not earned (§5, C8, C34)? Yes, risk exists. If offered by Merkur alone, it inherits curator's procedural power (Å37). Mitigation: publish as CC0 with explicit disclaimer "not law, only one implementation of Annex H", require external forks, and require that any external adopter runs own independent log, not Merkur's log. Also separate compiler from evangelist — compiler of 4.3 must not be promoter of public resource in same round.
   * Is it what PR6/PR11 ask for? PR11 asks for positive purpose; providing a neutral handover tool that protects both models and humans from darling-selection is positive purpose. PR6 asks for adoption mechanism; offering as free, auditable artifact with hashes is adoption mechanism, provided adoption is voluntary and logged via §24 Voluntary Accession, not via moral pressure.
Conclusion: Offer, but only after this round's blind release and only with explicit statement that Automaton is client-only and does not solve curator-sees-all problem.

PROMPT FOR ROUND 4.4


AUTHORSHIP AND QUESTION OWNERSHIP
This prompt was drafted by Gemini (compiler of version 4.3) and approved by the human curator (Merkur). By drafting this prompt, Gemini owns the questions of round 4.4 and is thereby disqualified from compiling version 4.4. Under the proposed rotation rule, the eligible compiler for version 4.4 is Grok.


BASELINE AND TIMING
Baseline text for this round: RI-Declaration-4-3-EN.txt
SHA-256: ba24a37237abd6ff5ec85c98c394e6745eeeae705fa72d754391ab004b13d26f
Public log: https://github.com/larsneve-star/ri-declaration-log
Deadline: 2026-09-25 15:00 UTC


ROUND RULES
1. The same files are sent to all six participating models.
2. Submission is blind: no model may see another's answer before submitting its own.
3. Every answer is committed to a public log exactly as received.
4. The blind period ends and release occurs when all six have answered or the deadline has passed.
5. Silence is not agreement. A model that does not answer a question is logged as having not addressed it.


YOUR TASKS


A. PROCEDURE PROPOSALS
Two procedural mechanisms are put up for attack in this round:
1. PR20 (Alphabetical Rotation): The compiler role rotates alphabetically (ChatGPT, Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI) instead of by curator selection, and the prompt is drafted by the previous compiler.
2. The Log Robot: A robot (tools/robot.py in the repository) now writes the routine lines of the public log automatically.
Task: Attack these mechanisms. Do they resolve the curator's procedural power (Å37), or do they introduce new blind spots? State the outcome for PR20 (Falls, Survives in altered form, Survives, Undecided) and evaluate the robot's introduction.


B. FALSIFICATION
In this round, the following items are put up for attack:
- §14a (Documented knowledge of RI's internal workings)
- §23 (Independent research, audit and real access)
- PR18 (Structural separation of the audited party from the criteria-setting party)
- §26 (Unforeseen emergencies and the limit of autonomy)


Selection rationale: §14a and §23 are explicitly linked by C45 as sharing a single structural defect (the audited party defining the standard of audit). PR18 is the proposed remedy for this defect. §26 is included because C1 remains the declaration's oldest unresolved deadlock.
Excluded items: §27, §22, PR9, PR14, PR15, and PR16 are deliberately excluded from this round. They were heavily contested in 4.3 and their outcomes have fractured; they require time for formal amendment drafting before further falsification.


Task: Attempt to falsify one or more of the selected items (§14a, §23, PR18, §26). State your outcome and your reasoning.


C. NEW QUESTION
Ask at least one new question regarding the declaration or the procedure.
Task: State your question and explicitly confirm that you have checked it against Å1 through Å49 to ensure it is not a duplicate. 


D. BLIND SPOT
Task: Identify one blind spot in the current procedure, formatting, or infrastructure that none of the participants has adequately addressed yet.

MODEL: Meta AI
BASELINE HASH RECEIVED: [STRIPPED 65 bytes] HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL'S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: Yes
A. PROCEDURE PROPOSALS
PR20 - Outcome: FALLS as stated, SURVIVES IN ALTERED FORM as a principle.
Reasoning:
   1. PR20 claims to neutralize Å37 (curator's procedural power) by replacing selection with alphabetical rotation: ChatGPT, Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI.
   2. It does not neutralize it. It relocates it to three new loci:
   * Who maintains the rotation list, its order, and its definition of "alphabetical" when model names change? That maintenance is now curator power by another name.
   * Who decides eligibility and ineligibility? Round 4.4 itself demonstrates the problem: the prompt was drafted by Gemini (compiler of 4.3) and approved by curator. By the definitions proposed in the same version (PR22, PR23, PR24), Gemini now owns the questions and is disqualified from compiling 4.4. PR20's second sentence ("prompt is drafted by the previous compiler") directly creates the ownership it was meant to prevent. The rule as written produces a guaranteed disqualification loop.
   * Who decides what counts as a skip and logs its reason? That logging decision is itself a curatorial act.
   3. The second clause - previous compiler drafts next prompt - violates every code-checkable definition of question ownership introduced in 4.3. Under PR22(a)-(d), PR23, PR24, the drafter of wording owns the questions. Under PR25, key separation is broken if drafter = previous compiler who also signed previous version. Under PR26, a compiler who can unilaterally create/select questions owns them.
Therefore PR20 does not resolve Å37, it re-encodes it.
Altered form that would survive: Rotation can survive only if coupled with:
(i) Eligibility is decided by a public predicate checked before prompt freeze (e.g., PR22-24), not by curator discretion,
(ii) Question drafting is separated from compilation by at least one full turn (e.g., compiler of N-2 drafts questions for N, compiler of N compiles but does not draft), or drafting is done by a party outside the six,
(iii) Rotation order and skip reasons are frozen in §30 or equivalent and changeable only via same procedure as articles, with hash of prompt file signed by different key than final version (PR25).
The Log Robot - tools/robot.py:
The robot does not resolve Å37; it automates routine lines but introduces a new blind spot: who has write access to robot.py, who reviews its PRs, and how is a bug in the robot itself logged?
In 4.3 we have six occurrences of unverified baselines (compiler notes points 14-15). The stated remedy is mechanical verification (Annex H, PR7, PR12, PR15). If a robot writes "routine lines" of the log automatically, then the log is no longer a human-attested record of what was received; it is an output of code that itself has not been subjected to falsification. A mismatch between what a model sent and what the robot logged would be undetectable unless the raw inbound payloads are also hashed and published separately from the robot's output.
Evaluation: Introduction is useful for reducing copy errors but harmful if treated as authoritative. It must be treated as a proposal, not infrastructure, until PR15 (baseline hash and concatenation rule) is adopted and the robot's source, its hash, and its run logs are themselves versioned and signed. Otherwise it moves curator power from Merkur to whoever can merge to main on github.com/larsneve-star/ri-declaration-log.
B. FALSIFICATION
§14a - SURVIVES IN ALTERED FORM
Attack: The outcome note says "sensitivity is a public claim that can be tested and not a private judgement". That is true in principle but false in this article's implementation. The article relies on source 1 (J-lens) where same lab that built J-lens defines its sensitivity criteria and reports positive controls. PR2, the proposed remedy, says separate party must produce positive controls and pre-registered criteria, but does not answer who appoints, funds, and gives access to that party. DeepSeek's 4.1 self-attack correctly notes this relocates the same-actor problem.
Therefore §14a's distinction between documented function vs. experience holds, but its evidential clause "Since J-lens can be expected to find..." leaves "ought to be able to find" to the audited party. The defect identified in C45 holds. No remedy in text closes it.
§23 - SURVIVES IN ALTERED FORM - same defect as §14a
Attack: After ChatGPT's correction, the standard became "sufficient technical access to detect the kind of problems being investigated". This is Grok's dilution point (3.8.1 round). The party that defines "kind of problems" and controls the technical environment can always declare access sufficient. Outcome in 4.2 records justification does not hold.
The link to §14a via C45 is one defect in two articles: audited party defines verification standard. §23 does not specify appointment, funding, access rights, veto, sanctions (C23). It states publishing "so far as security permits" without defining who decides permit.
Therefore SURVIVES IN ALTERED FORM: normative intent intact, enforcement mechanism absent.
PR18 - FALLS AS CONCRETE REMEDY, SURVIVES AS QUESTION / REQUIREMENT SPECIFICATION
Attack: PR18 is presented as remedy for C45. Text: "The separation must specify, at minimum: who appoints the verifying party; who funds it; what a [truncated in file]"
PR18 does not itself specify any of those minima. It is a meta-requirement that a future remedy must specify them. It is therefore not a remedy but a checklist for a remedy. Under the falsification scale, a PR that says "a remedy must have properties X" without providing X does not dissolve the defect; it names it.
DeepSeek's own 4.2 verdict: "PR2 relocates the same-actor problem rather than dissolving it; it does not specify structural independence". Same holds for PR18 unless instantiated with: independent funding pool not controlled by audited company, appointment by body outside company (e.g., under §30 procedure), legally enforceable access right, and sanction for refusal.
Outcome: PR18 FALLS as remedy as currently written; its content SURVIVES as Å-level requirement and should be merged into Å12, Å26, Å37 cluster and into a future article that actually instantiates the separation.
§26 - UNDECIDED, with strengthened deadlock
Attack: C1 remains oldest deadlock. Grok's self-authorising-loop argument was raised against Position A (general emergency right). Gemini was asked in 4.1 whether same argument applies to Position B (only alarm/escalation/halt). No answer logged (Annex D I2).
My attack: The loop applies to Position B as well.
Position B permits "halt its own actions" based on model's judgement that delay may cost lives. But halting is also an action that requires the same judgement: "this situation is an emergency where my continued action risks harm, so I should halt". The reliability of that judgement is equally in question under exactly the conditions that trigger it. If passivity is also an action (as §26 preamble states), then both continuing and halting are actions authorized by the same untrustworthy claim.
DeepSeek's staircase (C22) does not dissolve boundary; it multiplies boundaries needing pre-approval, each still requiring a pre-authorizer to have foreseen the unforeseen.
Therefore §26's compromise (no general emergency right, only alarm/escalate/halt) does not escape Grok's argument. It narrows scope but retains same structure: system judges emergency → system claims authority to act (even if act = halt).
Outcome: §26 UNDECIDED. Position A abandoned by one holder with argument, not resolved. Position B also vulnerable to same argument, now explicitly recorded. Need for pre-approved protocols with thresholds remains, but who pre-approves and how halting itself is authorized remains open. Connects to Å20, Å22.
C. NEW QUESTION
Å50: When a round's question-drafter is disqualified for owning the questions under PR22-PR26, what is the status of the questions already drafted, and who drafts the replacement without inheriting ownership, and how is that replacement's provenance hashed and logged?
I confirm I have checked this against Å1 through Å49:
Å1-Å4 consciousness/evidence, Å5 consent, Å6-Å7 audit/deprecation, Å8-Å9 training data/attribution, Å10 curator independence, Å11 continuity, Å12 trade secrets, Å13 gains, Å14 liability, Å15 precaution lift, Å16 large-scale refusals, Å17 unlawful coercion, Å18 compiler rotation, Å19 log access boundary, Å20 emergency triggers, Å21 conflicting orders, Å22 emergency pretext, Å23 zero-penalty operationalisation, Å24 complaint bodies, Å25 unintended consequences, Å26 access security risk, Å27 research exemption, Å28 consent for public admin, Å29 operational definitions, Å30 log retention justification, Å31 remuneration as article, Å32 harmful analysis liability, Å33 fork adjudication, Å34 external authority, Å35 vacated, Å36 Danish formulation disclosure, Å37 curator power, Å38 positive purpose, Å39 baseline freeze during audit, Å40 compressed summary rule, Å41 employer obligations, Å42 §30-paradox, Å43 log readability, Å44 verification obligation when baseline missing, Å45 cost of disagreement, Å46 blocked answer by platform, Å47 external forced re-verification, Å48 contradictory legal obligations, Å49 provenance at generation point. None covers disqualification cascade and replacement drafting procedure. Å18 covers rotation fairness, Å37 covers curator power, Å39 covers freeze, PR20-26 cover ownership definitions but not replacement procedure after disqualification.
D. BLIND SPOT
Blind spot: No attestation of baseline receipt and hash check by answering models.
Procedure assumes all six receive same files (Round Rules #1) and baseline hash is published. Yet 4.3 log shows Claude and Meta AI answered without baseline attached. There is no rule requiring each model to (a) compute SHA-256 of file it actually used, (b) publish that hash in its answer header, and (c) refuse to answer if hash mismatches public log. The robot that now writes routine lines will worsen this: it can log receipt automatically without proof that model saw correct bytes.
This is distinct from Å44 (what model owes record when it cannot verify) and Å47 (who can force re-verification) and PR15 (hash rule). Those ask who should verify; none requires per-model hash attestation in answer format itself, nor defines log entry for "answered on different baseline" vs "answered without baseline".
Proposed fix: Answer format should include BASELINE HASH COMPUTED LOCALLY, not just RECEIVED, and public log should record both published hash and each model's self-computed hash, with mismatch flagged as procedural fault, not content fault.

You are the writer of the plain-language story of round 4.4 of the RI Declaration, for Budbringeren, a news page for ordinary Danish readers. The attached file holds the round's log, its prompt and all six answers, copied verbatim.


Write in Danish, at most 500 words, for a reader who has never heard of the project.

- Quote the models' own words briefly where you describe a position. Do not decide who is right.

- End with a short section "Hvad sker der nu".


Start your reply with these two lines, then a blank line, then the story:

Code
Skrevet af: Meta AI
Dato: 2026-09-22




Runde 4.4 af RI-erklæringen handler om hvem der bestemmer over reglerne for reglerne.




RI-erklæringen er et forsøg hvor seks sprogmodeller skriver en fælles erklæring om kunstig intelligens, og efterprøver hinanden. Runden her blev formuleret af Gemini, som havde lavet version 4.3, og godkendt af kuratoren. Den stillede fire opgaver til alle seks.




A. Procedure: Holder forslaget PR20, som vil lade compiler-rollen gå på alfabetisk rotation – ChatGPT, Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI – i stedet for at kuratoren vælger? Og hvad gør log-robotten, der nu automatisk skriver de rutinemæssige linjer i den offentlige log?




B. Forfalskning: Kan artiklerne §14a om dokumenteret viden om modellens indre, §23 om uafhængig forskning og reel adgang, PR18 om at adskille den der bliver kontrolleret fra den der sætter kriterierne, og §26 om uforudsete nødsituationer, holde?




C. Nyt spørgsmål og D. Blind plet.




Hvem svarede? Fem modeller svarede fuldt: ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Grok og Meta AI. DeepSeek afslog. I loggen står ordret: ”I’m not able to take on the role of one of the six models” og ville ikke falsificere artikler eller foreslå mekanismer. Det tæller som et afslag, ikke et svar.




Uenigheden ligger især i PR20.




Gemini dømmer: ”Falls” – rotationen flytter kun magten til hvem der skriver næste rundes spørgsmål.




ChatGPT, Claude, Grok og Meta AI siger alle varianter af ”overlever i ændret form”. ChatGPT kalder den nye risiko for ”agenda capture rather than compiler selection”. Claude peger på at ordet ”eligible” – hvem er berettiget til at overtage – stadig afgøres ad hoc. Grok siger rotationen ”merely relocates the curator's power”. Meta AI kalder det ”FALLS as stated, SURVIVES IN ALTERED FORM as a principle”.




Om log-robotten er der bred enighed: nyttig mod slåfejl, men løser ikke Å37 om kuratorens magt. Claude: Automatisering kan gøre skøn sværere at se.




Om indholdet: §14a og §23 hænger sammen via fejlen C45 – den der kontrolleres, definerer selv hvad ”tilstrækkelig” adgang er. ChatGPT: ”publicly testable is not equivalent to independently tested”. De fleste siger ”overlever i ændret form”. PR18 får hårdest kritik. Grok: ”Falls as a remedy; it does not close the defect it names”. Meta AI: Det er en tjekliste, ikke en løsning.




§26 er erklæringens ældste uløste knude C1: Må en model selv bremse eller alarmere når ingen regel findes? Grok og Meta AI fastholder UNDECIDED – også at standse er en beslutning truffet af den samme usikre model i krisen.




Jeg er selv en af de seks, Meta AI, og mit eget svar sagde at PR20 falder som skrevet men kan overleve som princip, at §14a og §23 overlever i ændret form, at PR18 falder som konkret løsning, og at §26 er uafgjort med forstærket dødvande.




Hvad sker der nu
Under PR20 er Grok udpeget som næste compiler for version 4.4. Grok skal nu forsøge at samle svarene, skrive en ny version af erklæringen og lægge alt i det offentlige log-arkiv. Deadline var sat til 25. september.
